# LLP 3000.000.000.000: Release-spanning clients and development contracts

**Type:** RFC
**Status:** Implemented
**Systems:** Deployment, Client, Codegen, CLI, Testing
**Author:** Codex for Charlie Cheever (REL3)
**Date:** 2026-10-14
**Revised:** 2026-10-16
**Related:** [LLP 3000.000.000](./3000.000.000-retained-storage-and-hot-release.rfc.md), [LLP 3000.011.003.000](./3000.011.003.000-offline-ready-application-starters.rfc.md), [LLP 3000.036](./3000.036-what-the-app-farm-asks-of-the-rule-language.rfc.md)

## 1. Commission

Charlie commissioned wave-12 REL3 for 0.4.29: one frontend across release
rollback, operator safeguards, deterministic fixture assertions, development
hints and the first-check Exact rebuild instruction. Implementer: Codex,
2026-10-14. This amends the parent release RFC's client compatibility recipe;
retention, authorization, adoption, sync and expiry authority are unchanged.

## 2. Served capabilities

The authenticated `GET /` catalog adds each operation's public argument shape
and each public table's public field shapes, from the same compiler projection
as `/schema`. No rule/program body, private field or stored row is added.
Operation existence is not authorization. Jobs remain operator-only.

Clients expose `release`, a subscription source, and React `useRelease()`.
A snapshot is loading, denied, or an immutable served generation with operation
and table maps. It is scoped to current credentials; signout, close and retired
app facades withdraw it. Subscriptions fetch the catalog even if all query gates
are closed, and poll at one second to detect quiet switches. Ordinary response
generation headers withdraw older metadata immediately and request a refresh.
Requests are bounded, serialized and discarded if their credentials changed;
responses below the highest observed generation cannot reinstall an old snapshot.
Offline/catalog failure withdraws to loading. Metadata never licenses a write;
callers still handle E_OP/E_INPUT and authored guard refusals in a switch race.

## 3. Generated compatibility

Generated operation handles gain `available(release)`: true only when that
operation's name, kind and canonical argument shape equal the generated handle.
Unready metadata returns false. Generated TypeScript modules are self-contained
at runtime: package imports are type-only. The canonical availability check is
emitted once per api.ts, with the same semantics as the SDK helper. Generated
server.ts accepts the application's imported startServer as its first argument;
the application owns runtime dependencies. This conservative check gates workflows whose
arguments changed; it does not assert that business semantics are unchanged.

Applications opt in with `snapback/client-compatibility.json`:
`{"baseline":"versions/v1","since_release":2}`. The baseline is a directory
of .q files relative to the project root, outside snapback/, preserving original
filenames and module identities. A single-file baseline also preserves its
original filename; a complete root source must be named schema.q. Schema-first
ordering matches the project compiler. Empty directories and invalid sources
refuse without replacing generated API types. The baseline must compile. Saved-source deployment resolves this configuration
and baseline at the original project root while writing contracts only into the
private candidate. Missing baselines name their absolute path, configuration and
resolution root. The shared layout is versions/v1/schema.q (original-name modules
beside it), with contemporary fixtures in versions/v1/fixtures/. Both --schema
and --source accept the version directory; single complete .q inputs remain
supported. Compatibility output deduplicates merged enum and equal value domains.
The release number is an author-supplied documentation label, not an inferred
server generation. check and dev preserve this configuration when regenerating.
Without it, existing requiredness remains. Entirely new tables retain required
row fields: the baseline cannot return a row of an absent table. Added row fields and projected result
fields become optional and carry a Since release label; older value domains
remain in result unions. Baseline nominal rows/views use separate emitted
BaselineRows/BaselineWriteRows definitions, so removed tables still typecheck.
Fields private in either source remain optional. Declared optional arguments
remain optional with or without opt-in; the Since label marks only additions.
Parameters added to an existing operation become
optional; availability still rejects a changed signature. New operations retain
their own required arguments. Rows and typed projections use the same baseline.
Keep form drafts above components keyed by release generation and gate only the
release-specific reads/actions. Render optional fields with their baseline
fallback. A dormant row is absent in this release, not proof of deletion.

## 4. Rollback safeguards

Wave-13 grouping, effect ordering and snapshot interpretation amend this section
in [the parent §13](./3000.000.000-retained-storage-and-hot-release.rfc.md#13-wave-13-preview-decisions).

`rollback --check` performs the existing exact activation on its private coherent
snapshot, then relates dormant **row** inventories to the selected operations'
read tables, including read-rule dependencies. It names the selected release,
operation, table, dormant row count and bounded id samples. This is a conservative
dependency warning: it does not claim the dormant row would match an operation's
predicate or prove a business invariant. Existing column/index inventory units
stay distinct. Removed tables and operations retain their existing warnings.

An operator requiring a safety gate across rollback deploys a bridge that keeps
the gate's table, value domains, read audience and mutation guard; repairs or
migrates values while that guard still executes; proves refusal for the guarded
case; only then contracts source. Restoring storage cannot supply absent logic.

## 5. Fixtures and dev evidence

Case args, exact data and subset match assertions recursively resolve saved
aliases, retaining $name -> row id and $name.field semantics and literal escapes.
Unresolved aliases or paths fail with E_FIXTURE_ALIAS naming the alias. match
subsets object keys recursively; arrays require equal length and order, with
recursive element matches; scalars are exact. data remains an exact assertion.

Typed time leaves in fixture rows and case arguments accept now and now±a
positive integral duration in ms/s/m/h/d/w. One timestamp is captured per test
invocation for engine activation, seed, cases and the query sweep. Other text
fields are untouched. Malformed/overflowed times fail E_FIXTURE_TIME. Cases run
in authored order before the derived query sweep so their writes are visible.
Sweep derived program-require refusals are probes, not affirmative coverage;
explicit case expectations still control case pass/fail.

An empty viewer-keyed roster that caused a development refusal gets an empty
roster/seed hint in the private dev console, based on that engine's actual rows.
Production and the application refusal envelope gain no row-count information.
This amends LLP 3000.015 §2.1 only for host diagnostic preparation: one bounded
physical existence probe per referenced roster per request, cached across rule
and program sites. The trace collector itself still performs no reads or rule
reevaluation; the probe neither changes authorization nor the query meter.
The testing guide documents raw /seed arrays, /dev/reset wrappers and clock
advance CLI/HTTP forms. KIT owns proof-kit sections; LANG owns generic sweep
refusal wording; RULES2 owns authorization/refusal recipes.

## 6. Upgrade evidence

check --fix repairs documented fixture refusal renames only where replay against
the compiled schema reproduces the new code; hidden conflicts are not renamed
on spelling alone. It preserves unrelated JSONL bytes. Upgrade deltas persist
per-query static completeness facts and compare actual snapshots. An old snapshot
without those facts is unknown, never a manufactured improvement.

FIX28's runtime history acknowledgment also prints
`Exact device: bun exact.mjs snapback --rebuild` once after a version change when
an Exact device mount is present. Ordinary output uses stdout, JSON mode stderr;
compiler failure does not repeat an acknowledged history interval. Online Exact
mounts get no device rebuild instruction. EX7 owns Exact2's runtime/session work.

## 7. Verification

Required: the recursive generated-modules.test.mjs fixture census (package types
only, runtime availability parity and all operation shapes); fixture alias/match/time positive and negative cases; runtime-confirmed
rename controls; completeness history/Exact acknowledgment; empty-roster dev-only
hint; catalog authentication/public projection and generation refresh without
queries; React account/generation withdrawal; generated baseline optionality and
availability; populated rollback dependency warning plus zero-row/control tables;
existing adoption and rollback suites. Run ref-check, docs budget, root inventory
and guide checks after the root dependency/artifact bootstrap. No full test.sh,
publication or push is authorized by this workstream.

## 8. Review dispositions

Round 1: Muse found a query-change-feed refusal unnecessarily gated the online
catalog; its current-identity predicate now excludes that query gate, with a real
client refusal/quiet-refresh regression. Fresh Codex found three codegen defects:
declared optional arguments became required, old nominal results referenced
removed Rows entries, and baseline-private fields became required after publicity.
All three now have focused generated-output and actual TypeScript regressions.
The baseline has its own row namespace; optionality includes both privacy maps.
Verbatim reviews retain their NOT READY verdicts. Further review is required.

Round 2: Muse READY; Codex found generation-floor initialization and lost baseline
module identities. Initial credentials and generation hints now preserve the
observed floor, including credential changes during an outstanding fetch.
Baselines now load an entire source directory in schema-first order, preserving
all module filenames; a single-file baseline retains its original module name.
Both paths have CLI regressions for module argument/result additions. Existing
complete-source examples save schema.q within the release directory. New tables
also retain required row fields, since the baseline cannot return their rows.

Round 3: both [Muse](./reviews/3000.000.000.000-rel3-r3.muse.md) and
[fresh Codex](./reviews/3000.000.000.000-rel3-r3.codex.md) mark
`77f1520bf4` READY with no material issue remaining. Codex disclosed that a late
RFC diff displayed §8 after its assessment had been reached; no reviewer files
were read. That reading-isolation limitation is preserved in its verbatim
artifact. Both sessions were fresh and used the commissioned model/effort and
commands. The implementation is complete; this status change records completion,
not author acceptance of an unimplemented proposal. Validation and explicit
platform/legacy skips are recorded in WORKSTREAM-REPORT.md; no push/publication.

### PUBFIX29b consumer repair (2026-10-16)

Exact's captured-module build rejected api.ts's runtime SDK import. Section 3
now makes every generated module self-contained at runtime: the availability
predicate is inline and the generated server wrapper takes the application-owned
runtime. A 60-schema recursive census checks imports and runtime parity across
all client operation shapes. Both supplied Exact apps pass their two web tests
with branch-generated types, including the device app's offline/reconnect case.

Round 1 [Muse](./reviews/3000.000.000.000-pubfix29b-r1.muse.md) was READY;
[fresh Codex](./reviews/3000.000.000.000-pubfix29b-r1.codex.md) found missed
server-wrapper type fixtures/assertion and guide prose. Those callers now supply
the runtime, including negative type witnesses. Round 2
[Muse](./reviews/3000.000.000.000-pubfix29b-r2.muse.md) and
[fresh Codex](./reviews/3000.000.000.000-pubfix29b-r2.codex.md) are both READY.
Verbatim artifacts preserve each review and its provenance. Version stays
0.4.29; the workstream report records verification and root bootstrap limits.

The full tests subsequently required preserving the exact Chromium diagnostic
phrase, keeping the retired installer out of generated-source narration, and
updating the old bare-usage assertion to require the replacement guidance.
Those validation corrections retain the guards. Final
[Muse](./reviews/3000.000.000.000-pubfix29b-r3.muse.md) and
[fresh Codex](./reviews/3000.000.000.000-pubfix29b-r3.codex.md) reviews are READY;
Codex records the need to rebuild the binary after the diagnostic wording edit.

### DAY3 amendment review record — 2026-10-16

Fresh [Codex r1](./reviews/3000.011.003.000.000-day3-r1.sol.md) and
[Codex r2](./reviews/3000.011.003.000.000-day3-r2.sol.md) were NOT READY.
Saved directories are now immutable through adaptation/re-init, every baseline
failure names its original resolution context, explicit saved fixtures never
skip the starter sample, and historical chat/social proofs retain their
assertions while gaining their build step. Physical read-through root retention
is preflighted before recovery writes. Each has a focused regression.

Fresh [Codex r3](./reviews/3000.011.003.000.000-day3-r3.sol.md) and
[Muse r3](./reviews/3000.011.003.000.000-day3-r3.muse.md) are both READY.
[Provenance](./reviews/3000.011.003.000.000-day3-r3.provenance.json) records
commands, hashes, blind-session isolation and Muse's disclosed comparison limit:
it inspected amendment text against code but reported not locating the base
ref. No base-diff review is claimed for Muse. Earlier Muse attempts returned no
usable review; the structured retry produced the saved verbatim artifact.
Readiness records the review outcome, not author acceptance of this RFC.

## 14. Wave 14 persona to production

**Implementer:** Codex for Charlie Cheever, 2026-10-16 (PROD workstream,
wave-14 fix list #8/#9). The commission explicitly authorizes this schema
adaptation and its tests. It supersedes §12's refusal of a production switch.

`init --adapt --production` is read-only by default; `--apply` writes the
complete displayed schema plan. It adds bare `use identity` only when no
module declares identity, keeping both password and guest accounts. Existing
identity methods stay explicit and unchanged. Guests POST `/auth/guest`
before any read, including a public query; `/auth/config` is GET. Choosing
password-only is an explicit schema edit, never an inferred conversion.

The planner shares N_NO_PRODUCTION_WRITER's least-fixed-point analysis and
lists all blocked tables, including blocked dependents. It recognizes a roster
as a table with a principal column in a unique key. For each blocked roster
without inherited `read through` authority, it emits one fresh-named,
service:ops-guarded insert mutation whose arguments include its writable
required fields and principal even when the principal defaults to viewer.
An enum role is an explicit typed argument; signup confers no role. It adds
the independent service:ops branch to that roster's read and insert rules,
retaining existing account branches, constraints and defaults. The preview
shows the exact resulting schema, including every grant. Non-roster roots
and inherited-authority roots are listed for application-specific policy;
no generic profile/directory policy is invented. The entire proposed program
must compile before apply can write anything. Repeated apply is idempotent
when those paths make the roster reachable. Apply preflights all source
bytes and refuses source symlinks; individual replacements use init's atomic
file replacement. A filesystem failure partway reports possible partial
application and does not claim a transaction across files.

Frontend source scanning reports file/line locations containing `dev:`;
it does not print or replace the source text. Dependencies, generated stores,
builds, fixtures, saved versions and recipe copies are excluded, symlinks are
not followed, and traversal has a bounded depth. Findings are review hints,
not proof of complete principal migration. No application screen or client,
fixture, package manifest, deployment or stored row is changed by this mode.
DAY4 owns ordinary init/adapt advice and proof/version handling; the production
plan is an independent module. EX9 owns the equivalent Exact recipe.

React and Expo starters carry a runnable account/persona recipe beside the
ordinary app: a declared-role roster, authorized principal picker, invitation
by shared principal and persisted password sign-in. Persona controls require
GET /auth/config development=true; they never encode a production role.
The recipe's account draft and selection owners sit above release-sensitive
query children. Missing/denied capabilities render not available; complete
empty lists render empty through absent. Default todo drafts follow the same
stable-owner shape. Account transitions retain AppProvider's intentional
principal boundary; release changes do not remount that boundary.

Client configuration failures for invalid writes values or combinations with
a device or readOnly become an inert signed-out App: ready resolves
{ok:false,why} and useAuth().why exposes the same E_CLIENT/createApp.writes.
Construction performs no identity, storage, device or network acquisition;
all sign-in methods remain refused until the app is recreated with corrected
configuration, and the client cannot read or write anonymously. Browser
prerequisite failures retain E_ADMISSION exceptions. This amends LLP
3000.013.000 §17's construction-refusal presentation without weakening its
no-storage/no-network requirement or introducing a new client state.

Acceptance: preview/apply/default-identity/idempotence/multiple-roster tests,
blocked-root enumeration, unchanged custom screens and fixtures, multiline
rule preservation, collision handling, no-write on failed compilation,
principal scan and symlink refusal; real service-seat writes and outsider
refusals after deploy. Packed-tarball qualification runs the new recipe on
React and Expo, tests helper argument shapes, exercises release rollback
with a draft and selection alive, denied roster and empty list states, and
runs production preview/apply on persona-only and wave-14 roster copies.
No production publication is claimed by these consumer checks.


§14 semantic reviews: [fresh Codex READY](./reviews/3000.011.003.000-wave14-prod-r1.sol.md)
and [Muse READY](./reviews/3000.011.003.000-wave14-prod-r1.muse.md), with
[provenance](./reviews/3000.011.003.000-wave14-prod-r1.provenance.json).
Nonblocking implementation advice was applied: structural independent seat
recognition, explicit defaulted role arguments, full-source preflight and
source-set comparison. The manual `why` proposals retain per-role literal
mutations; automatic adaptation uses one enum-typed mutation per roster.
Both are ordinary service-seat insert paths with the same authority policy.

### Expo acquisition addendum: Expo upgrade and adapter-first acquisition

Implementer: Codex, 2026-10-16. Upgrade reads the target adapter's Expo peer floor
before mutating the project. It selects that exact floor, or preserves a newer
exact patch in the same qualified SDK major, reads that Expo tarball's
bundledNativeModules.json, and exact-pins every declared package in that map.
For Router apps it also pins Reanimated and Worklets from that same map, even
when previously transitive: Router wildcard optional peers otherwise select
Worklets 0.13, incompatible with Expo core 57.0.23. The reference social starter
pins SDK 57.0.30's Reanimated 4.5.1 and Worklets 0.10.1 pair.
The target adapter's Expo-module peer floors override lower Expo recommendations
and are pinned even when newly required: Expo 57.0.22 recommends core 57.0.18,
but the adapter requires core 57.0.23. It writes all core/adapter/Expo pins
before one package-manager resolution so
peers in different dependency sections resolve together. Sections are preserved;
ambiguous duplicates and unsupported pin forms refuse before install. The plan
names the old/new Expo version and all aligned pins. Non-Expo upgrades retain
the existing package-manager acquisition path. The native floor decision is in
LLP 3000.030; init continues using the reference app's newer patch pins.

The README, guide expo and init next steps agree: init --expo, npm install,
check. Adapter-first acquisition uses an exact RN 0.86.3 peer and mirrors that RN
release's React ^19.2.3 peer. An exact React peer fails npm's greedy optional-web
peer selection in an empty project. Compatible React 19 may resolve during the
bootstrap-only install; init plus its normal npm install pins React and React DOM
to the native-qualified 19.2.3 set. No native React 19.3 claim follows. Qualification
checks the initial RN pin and compatible React range, then both exact React pins
and a web export after init.
The direct release install line is `npm install --save-exact snapback4@0.4.31
snapback4-expo@0.4.31`. Run `npx snapback4@latest upgrade` to acquire the new
helper from an older pinned CLI; an already installed older helper cannot be
repaired retroactively. Qualification first installs and generates the actual published 0.4.29 Expo
57.0.22 consumer and its lockfile, then exercises the explicitly acquired
candidate helper and the literal two-package install separately,
then typechecks and exports web with the resulting real npm tree. DAY4 owns
starter/adapt ownership advice; PROD owns account and release-aware UI recipes.


## 15. Wave 15 offline entry and production discovery

The AccountEntry guide recipe opens createApp immediately with persisted session
custody and the device. Auth configuration is optional discovery metadata,
keyed by backend URL, read defensively from localStorage and refreshed by GET
in the background. A missing cache defaults to accounts, not personas; a failed
fetch or cache write never gates restoration. Cached development metadata is
not authority: existing loopback and server persona admission still apply.
AccountEntry owns and closes its app; abort and closed-response checks prevent
retired effects from publishing. H9 qualification executes this literal packed
guide recipe with a real password session and device, reloads with every
backend request refused and no auth-config cache, and requires held account
data, readiness and no alert.

## Summary

Snapback 4 is one of two parallel candidates for the successor to Snapback 2,
and the sibling of Snapback 3, not its successor. It builds
[LLP 2000.000](./2000.000-keep-the-core-flip-two-substrates.rfc.md) as
written: the Snapback 2 core kept verbatim, two substrates flipped up front —
resources metered at runtime instead of priced statically, and a client that
replicates the viewer's declared partition instead of caching receipted
results — proved first on the hub fixture, with a deterministic simulator as
the primary harness. Snapback 3 ([LLP 2000](./2000-snapback3.rfc.md), led by
Codex / GPT-6 Astra) keeps the same two questions open and discovers their
answers in its first slice. Snapback 2 ([LLP 1000](./1000-snapback2.explainer.md))
continues as the incumbent and the control. All three are judged by one
pre-registered comparison on one clock.

This document records the author's decisions of 2026-09-12 that created the
track, restates the bet on one page so a reader can orient without the brief,
fixes the bakeoff protocol, draws the fence, and plans the first four weeks.
The design argument and its evidence stay in LLP 2000.000. Only running code
will bind (`rules/RULES.md`).

## 1. Decisions (author, 2026-09-12)

On 2026-09-12 Charlie Cheever asked whether Claude Fable 5.1 should lead a
Snapback 4 while Codex / GPT-6 Astra leads Snapback 3 and Snapback 2
continues, "and see which one turns out best." The lead answered yes with
conditions; the author accepted them ("Sounds good") and asked for this
document. The decisions below are therefore the lead's proposal as accepted
by the author. Where the author has not separately ruled, the text says so.

1. **Three tracks, one comparison.** Snapback 2 continues as the incumbent
   and the control. Snapback 3 builds LLP 2000; Snapback 4 builds LLP
   2000.000. This is a bakeoff of *bets* — discover the substrates in the
   slice versus decide them first — not of models. The result will say more
   about the bets than about the leads, and §3 is written so that it can.
2. **The name is Snapback 4.** Siblings, not a sequence: "4" does not mean
   "after 3." A separate number gives the track its own corpus root,
   directory, package identity and data directory with no rename when the
   bakeoff resolves, and files never move in LLP. If Snapback 4 loses, it is
   a number that never shipped. "Snapback 3b" was considered and rejected:
   it reads as a variant of 3, and this is a different bet.
3. **Snapback 4 builds LLP 2000.000 as written.** Its §14 open questions are
   the lead's to decide inside the track, recorded in §7 as they are
   decided. The author rules on the bar, the comparison and promotion (§3).
4. **One owner on the core.** The lead writes the engine, the store and the
   client contract. Codex lanes build the fixture generator, the reference
   applications, the device witnesses and the periphery. Blind reviews from
   two model families are reserved for the two disclosure-sensitive
   mechanisms: partition inversion of read rules (LLP 2000.000 §7.1) and
   the meter's cut behaviour on non-uniform scans (LLP 2000.000 §6). This
   departs from the standing rule that Codex codes and Claude orchestrates,
   deliberately, for the core only.
5. **The clock is four weeks** from the first commit under `snapback4/` to
   the first slice running on the hub fixture. At the clock the author kills
   the track, merges its substrates into a survivor, or continues it. There
   is no third month without a ruling.
6. **Shared and not shared.** Shared with Snapback 3: the LLP corpus, the
   Clique harness and scorers, the fixtures, the briefs, and the simulator
   once either track has one. Not shared: code, except the Snapback 2 pieces
   both are expected to copy (LLP 2000.000 §5.1).
7. **Snapback 2 keeps shipping and freezes new substrate work for the
   duration.** Fix-forward, the loop bar, acquisition and papercuts continue;
   no new certificate axes, client custody mechanisms or ceiling changes.
   This is accepted here as a condition of the bakeoff. Making it binding on
   concurrent Snapback 2 sessions needs one line in `rules/NOT-DOING.md`'s
   Moves ledger, which this document does not write.
8. **Reading load.** This root, updated in place, and one append-only
   journal, `snapback4/JOURNAL.md`. Sub-LLPs only for a decision that must
   outlive the journal. No panels beyond decision 4's two reviews.

## 2. The bet on one page

**Keep verbatim from Snapback 2** (LLP 2000.000 §5.1): the rule language
with its uniformity and hoist analysis, `decided_true` and the
structural-fact refusals; one data language, Quarry, with `--` comments,
`require … else CODE`, `shape`, `merge`, `walk`, `chunk`; the value model
and its conformance corpus; typed read and write states with no silent state
and the refusal envelope `{code, family, site, message, rewrite, guide}`; the
successor classifier (widening additive, checked narrowing, exact projection
ownership); `deliver`'s engine-computed audience, tombstones and
`bootstrap`; `schedule`, `job`, `chunk` and rehearsal; personas; `init`,
`dev`, `check`, `doctor`, `run`, `test`, `why`, `trace`, `guide`; H0
`deploy`, `rollback`, `secret`; channels; assets; the word-prefix index.

**Flip 1 — certify authorization, meter resources** (LLP 2000.000 §6). The
certificate keeps every read site's shape, uniformity, completeness class
and prediction eligibility, and stops pricing bytes and steps. A
deterministic meter charges examined rows, index entries, rule probes,
steps, heap, output and rows written as the program runs, on the server and
in the client interpreter alike. A query cut by the meter returns `capped`
with no continuation past the cut, the same observable outcome as a static
ceiling cut today; a mutation aborts with `E_BOUND` naming actual work; a
job is chunked. `check --cost` prints the structural cost class; `test`
prints measured meters on the hub fixture.

**Flip 2 — the viewer's partition is the unit of sync** (LLP 2000.000 §7).
`sync to <audience> last N by <index>` on a table makes the engine maintain,
per principal, the rows that principal may read, as a change log with a
horizon; a table without the word is online-only, and `check` says which
rule term prevented inversion. The client holds a replica of its partitions,
one watermark each, a durable outbox with client-minted ids, and the
interpreter; queries run locally and are `complete` inside the horizon.
Revocation is a scope tombstone; `until-revoked` and `delivered-history`
survive as the client's leave policy. Two client tiers — online-only
without a store, local-first with one — share one language and one server.

**The vocabulary is fixed before the mechanism** (LLP 2000.000 §8):

```ts
type Read<T> = { data: T; complete: boolean; fresh: boolean; since?: number; next?: Cursor }
            | { loading: true } | { denied: Refusal };
row.pending: boolean;
type Write = { state: "pending" } | { state: "sent"; seq: number } | { state: "failed"; why: Refusal };
type Link = "online" | "offline" | "signed-out";
```

A screen that needs a word not on that card is a defect in the mechanism,
not a gap in the card.

**The hub comes first** (LLP 2000.000 §11): the Clique generator's `u0001`
with 1,000 memberships, `u0500` with 500 follows, and about 100,000
messages are the first fixture; day-two changes are designed before the
day-one screen; the native client is in slice one; physical floors are
measured before targets; the simulator is the primary harness.

## 3. The bakeoff protocol

Shared with Snapback 3; LLP 2000 or a sibling record should adopt the same
text so that the two tracks are measured by one instrument.

**Brief.** [`benchmarks/briefs/social-messenger.md`](../benchmarks/briefs/social-messenger.md)
(Clique) M1–M8, plus
[`benchmarks/briefs/messaging-day2-evolution.md`](../benchmarks/briefs/messaging-day2-evolution.md).
The field-work application (LLP 2000 §4) tests generality after the first
slice, on whichever tracks survive the clock.

**Fixture.** The Clique generator at the instrument freeze LLP 1023 and
LLP 1024 record (`f40559f37722c79178be664a11f8a6485f1508fd`): hub `u0001`
with 100 groups and 900 DMs, `u0500` with 500 follows, and the 100,015
# What Snapback 4 Does Not Do

Longer than the doing-list on purpose. Moving something off it means writing one
line naming what it unblocks and taking something off the doing-list in the same
change. Nothing comes off during slice one (LLP 3000 §3: the clock is four weeks).

## The bars

The bakeoff gates in [LLP 3000 §3](../llp/3000-snapback4.explainer.md) are the
bars: the loop bar, hub acquisition within 2× the measured floor, client bytes
under the competitor-anchored budgets, the six scenarios green in the simulator
on the hub fixture. Everything not required to pass them does not exist yet.

## Not building

- **A second authoring language.** Quarry, one spelling, copied from Snapback 2 (LLP 1004, LLP 1007 §4b). TypeScript is for seeds, journeys, effect bodies and the client.
- **Byte- and step-priced certification.** No closure, heap, output or step ceilings at `check`; the certificate certifies shape, uniformity, completeness class and prediction eligibility. Resources are metered at runtime (LLP 2000.000 §6).
- **A per-query result cache.** No range receipts, validators, halos, absence evidence, coverage spans, extent facts, held directories, fact integrity or swap journals. The client replicates declared partitions with one watermark each (LLP 2000.000 §7).
- **Engine-minted ids for client inserts, grants, ID slices.** Ids are client-minted, time-ordered, 128-bit; the engine mints for its own writes.
- **The `drain` protocol, follower-tab inbox rows, `defineItems`, `withHistory`, `mutationTarget`, `receipt.source`, `local.caughtUp`, `partial` reason codes.** The card (`packages/snapback4/src/contract.ts`) is the whole client vocabulary; `why` carries the rest.
- **`retain` as a receipt rule.** `until-revoked` and `delivered-history` survive as the client's leave policy on a scope tombstone.
- **JavaScript in the data plane.** Queries and mutations run in the Rust interpreter inside the SQLite transaction, and in the same interpreter on the client (host decided at week 3 by measurement, LLP 2000.000 §9). A *job* may be the app's own Rust, hosted as wasm through the interpreter's operations under the job's meter (LLP 3000.005, the Moves line below); a query or mutation may not.
- **An embedded JS engine for effects.** The data plane never calls out. `emit` writes the effect durably in the mutation's commit and an external worker holding a service token claims, performs and settles it over the wire (`snapback4/worker`); the four reference effects run on the builder's Node (LLP 2000.000 §10, decided 2026-09-13).
- **Committed generated code; an auth mode; a supervised bundler.** As Snapback 2 (`rules/NOT-DOING.md`): types by inference, personas not modes, `SNAPBACK_URL` is the client contract.
- **A TypeScript twin of the interpreter.** Retired by the author's ruling of 2026-09-13 (LLP 3000 §7): one implementation, in Rust, on every host — the core's SQLite under the native module, memory under wasm on the web with the host persisting each commit's changes. The web pays 312,924 B gzip before the diet where the twin's tier paid 16,842; the conformance obligation between two interpreters ends. Expo Go runs the online tier.
- **More than one hosted target, an admin console, media transcoding or thumbnails, push delivery itself (a worker's), cron syntax beyond `every N at HH:MM`, vector or relevance-ranked search (word-prefix search is in: `words(..)` and `matching`), federation, multi-region, device-to-device sync, at-rest encryption claims.** After the clock, on evidence.
- **General observability beyond `why`, `trace`, `logs`, `data`, `sql`, server operation latency, and bounded client subscription inspection.** The `sql` verb is read-only inspection of the developer's own store (LLP 3000.007 §8.8), never an application surface. Separately declared application SQL reads are admitted for increment D (LLP 3000.009 §6.2, §14.4; the 2026-09-16 Moves line below), not implemented by this inspection verb.
- **A guide corpus.** The compiler's facts, one language card and keyword cards, as Snapback 2; no freshness dates. Every card's Quarry is compiled by the suite and the language card is run, so a card cannot promise a form the parser refuses.

- **Partial union delivery, two group audiences, a public branch.** A union syncs principal-column branches and at most one group branch, each a whole disjunct of the read rule (LLP 3000.036.000.000); a branch with an extra condition, a second group or a `public` branch is refused with the rewrite, never partially delivered; service grants add no device audience (LLP 3000.007 §20.6). A union does not combine with delivered history, read-through children, synced or row-keyed views, owner keys, timelines, private field owners, acquisition windows or direct/inherited expiry on content or membership scopes.
- **Exact horizon maintenance.** The horizon bounds acquisition (a snapshot, a bootstrap); the log never evicts or back-fills to hold a device's group at exactly N rows. A local read past the acquired horizon by key says `complete: false`.

- **Clock expressions in rules.** A read rule compares with `=` and `!=` only; `.expiresAt > now` remains a mutation `require`. Stored lifetimes use `not before .col` for server-controlled opening and `expire at .col` for expiry. The engine publishes opening commits; devices receive no unopened payload. The supported compositions and explicit exclusions are in LLP 3000.036.003.
- **A jobs framework.** No priorities, worker pools or dead letters: one FIFO of runs, one chunk at a time, a refused chunk parked with its refusal. Actions, effects and scheduled actions carry per-operation retries, deadlines and cancellation, and a record per external step (LLP 3000.009 §5.2; the Moves line below). Missed period boundaries while a server is down fire once on return, not once per missed period.
- **Asset processing.** No transcoding, thumbnails, rungs or HEIC: the bytes are served as uploaded; JPEG, PNG, WebP and MP4 only; image dimensions and optional MP4 duration read from the header; MP4 dimensions are not extracted.
- **Search beyond word prefixes.** One tokenizer (NFC, lowercase, alphanumeric words), one index shape (`scope, words(cols), order.., id`), one read (`matching`): every term a prefix of some word, the first term's words merged as lanes, bounded by the meter in a uniform scope, at most 32 words in an admitted non-uniform scope. No stemming, no ranking by relevance, no phrase or mid-word matching, no per-language folding, no intersection index for the second term (it filters the page).
- **An identity provider.** Guests, password accounts, service seats, and sessions a service mints for accounts the application authenticated itself. No phone, OAuth, magic links or MFA in the engine: an application's worker verifies those and mints the session.
- **No multi-server presence, no presence history or last-seen storage, no broadcast event stream, no connection-bound rows.** `connected[p]` stays server-wide, counting held polls and their 35 s grace; a live query that may read it re-runs in the background about every 5 seconds to stay current (LLP 3000.018). Typing and viewing use short-lived, authorized ephemeral tables in one server process (LLP 3000.017); they never sync or decide durable invariants. A device does not know presence and says so.

## Process not doing

- **2026-09-28 — ordinary mixed search** (LLP 3000.028 §3.3, lane OS round 1).
  Admit the SDK's bounded merge of current server rows and certified Left images
  for the proved single time-ordered word page. This removes application-owned
  online/held selection for that shape. One server call per requested page;
  recomputation pays again from consumed positions. The coordinator shares the
  existing device read allowance. No durable search session, extra synced rows,
  new authority or additional allowance is admitted.

- **2026-09-28 — inferred current-scope word search** (LLP 3000.028 §3.3,
  lane OS). A single bare ordinary word page may derive the exact non-expiring
  membership audience for the existing compact server plan. This removes the
  requirement to repeat that audience predicate in this narrow server query.
  The compiler supplies a scoped words index when absent; it is an ordinary
  schema index, maintained on writes and on devices, with no extra synced rows
  or new authority. The device keeps its original bare scan. Round 0 left ordered composition
  and combined accounting outside its independent subset; round 1 above admits
  that bounded SDK composition. Durable search sessions remain excluded.
- **2026-10-12 — union sync audiences** (LLP 3000.036.000.000, workstream UNION, two blind review rounds). A table syncs to the union of principal-column audiences and one group audience; a device holds a row while any branch admits its viewer. What it unblocks: owner-or-team, buyer-or-seller and owner-or-staff tables offline for every reader, the shape three app farms asked for. Taken off: "one `sync` audience per table". Partial delivery, two groups and a public branch stay excluded (above).
- **2026-09-28 — explicit owner-key coverage** (LLP 3000.028 §3.4, Lane OK commission). A public window may opt into a compiler-owned, horizon-free principal key partition to answer ordinary own-key existence offline. Taken off: the exclusion of narrower platform key distribution beside one public window. General union audiences, full own-row replication and per-query absence caches remain excluded. The projection uses existing metered maintains, adoption and whole-partition publication; existing windows do not expand.
- **2026-09-26 — a live query keeps presence current** (LLP 3000.018). A 0.4.2 probe found an observed `connected[p]` reporting `fresh: true` while stale for over a minute, and a comparative builder polled around it. What it unblocks: online marks and presence-aware lists that stay current. Taken off: "a presence stream" as an exclusion for live queries; a presence API, last-seen storage and multi-server presence stay out. Under the author's standing authorization for reworks that make queries excellent (2026-09-25).

- **No drift registries, inventories, line-pinned diagnostics registries, claim digests or frozen-record gates.** Generated projections are built, not committed.
- **No gate bound to an exact commit; no push gated on a battery.** Five blocking checks in sixty seconds, run before push; everything slower on a schedule with bounded tickets.
- **No ceiling raised in slice one.** Budgets are anchored to a competitor's measured number and recorded once in the journal.
- **No standing panel** except the two blind reviews LLP 3000 decision 4 names (partition inversion; the meter's cut on non-uniform scans). The author explicitly commissioned the one-off LLP 3000.008 panel and disjoint Astra implementation lanes on 2026-09-14; its bounded disposition is recorded in that umbrella and LLP 3000 §7.
- **No new LLP number** without first extending LLP 3000 or LLP 2000.000; no spec without running code (`rules/RULES.md`: only implemented specs bind).
- **No second session on the core** during slice one; lanes take the periphery.
- **No test matrix as the primary harness.** The simulator is; real-browser and device witnesses cover only what it cannot reach.
- **No files over 1,500 lines**, including ones copied from Snapback 2. Split on
  the way in. The one exception is the package's published documents
  (`packages/snapback4/README.md` and its linked references): the rule exists to
  keep a *source* file reviewable, and the 0.2.25 calibration measured the cost of
  applying it to a document — splitting the README was that run's single largest
  scoring loss, because the builder then had four places to look instead of one
  (LLP 3000.007 §20.19). A published document is bounded by whether a builder can
  find one way in, not by a line count; the census that enforces this rule covers
  source, and says so.

## Deliberately worse

- No compatibility with Snapback 1 or Snapback 2 apps, packages or on-disk formats.
- No API stability before the clock; every publish is `0.0.x`.
- Sparse prose documentation; the diagnostics and the recipes carry the load.
- One hosted target, SQLite in the production CLI, one platform binary at a time. A separately enabled Postgres evaluation adapter is not a hosted/deployable target (Moves below).

## Moves

- **2026-10-13 — stored lower lifetimes (`not before`) are commissioned by PAGE2** (LLP 3000.036.003). This unblocks timed disclosure without an application publication job. Per-application jobs that flip a publication flag leave this admitted path; clock expressions in rules, captured lower lifetimes for read-through children, and boundary recounts for maintained data remain excluded. HS owns handshake transport; PAGE2 declares the new semantic capability.

- **2026-10-06 — exact Following history (FH delegated round-1 ruling).**
  Admit a Following-specific server traversal and bounded volatile frontier/replay
  state under LLP 3000.026 §9. The approved design reads exact history online
  from the first page; the SDK will own continuation. The first milestone is internal event metadata
  and the oracle, with no public completeness claim. Taken off: a blanket ban on
  state for this one expiring read. General result caches, resumable queries,
  device traversal, inbox coverage certificates and history-only blocks remain
  excluded. Existing inbox bytes and runtime ceilings remain unchanged.

- **2026-09-26 — server operation latency (LLP 3000.019 §3.7).** Calibration #22
  measured each read once and could not claim repeated p95/degradation testing.
  Unblocks percentile measurements of the application's own load without a
  custom timing harness. Taken off: a latency harness written by the builder;
  general metrics, tracing infrastructure and an admin console remain excluded.

- **2026-09-23 — Quarry states intent moves onto the doing-list** (Charlie:
  "if you need to try making *significant* architectural reworks in order to
  make the data modeling, queries, and scalability be excellent and elegant,
  you can experiment with that"; LLP 3000.013). Unblocks reads that state
  predicates and order, one `view` declaration for derived data, Following
  without caps, jobs and aggregates without author-chosen bounds, an app
  object and an offline web shell. Taken off: index names and author-chosen
  engine numbers on the paved path; the timeline's promotion, pull routes and
  caps; the "Time in rules" refusal is unchanged. Stands: one interpreter, no
  JavaScript in the data plane, no lowering Quarry to SQL, no relevance-ranked
  search.

- **2026-09-16 — the remaining general-purpose program moves onto the doing-list**
  (Charlie: "complete everything ... we'll remove services when we are done
  with the workroom test rather than wait to add them in ... use the fleet";
  LLP 3000.009 §14.7). Unblocks the §7 integrated-service baseline, C3 production
  Postgres, D's authorized database capabilities and measurement-led C2/E
  work while Workroom continues. Taken off: waiting for Workroom before
  starting services, and keeping production Postgres integration outside the
  current implementation program. The completed Workroom record drives a
  retain/remove pass. Stands: one interpreter and managed writer, no managed
  SQL writes, no JavaScript transactions, no SQL in the first Workroom cell,
  no invented performance/real-provider evidence, no expo-build-1000 load,
  and no new publication or managed-service deployment authority.

- **2026-09-16 — application SQL reads move onto the doing-list** (Charlie: "admit application SQL reads"; LLP 3000.009 §6.2, §14.4, increment D). Unblocks reporting and search over authorized projection inputs and declared database-owned tables, with authorization before selection, ordering and limits. Taken off in the same change: the inspection-only exclusion for application SQL and D's pending-author gate. Implementation and qualification remain pending; SQLite and Postgres halves ship together, channels follow candidates, and A1–A3 plus the fixture pack stay first. Stands: no SQL writes into managed data, no lowering Quarry to SQL, no SQL in the first Snapback brief cell, and no publication authorization.

- **2026-09-15 — a Postgres evaluation backend moves onto the doing-list** (author: "Build the evaluation prototype"). Unblocks concrete evaluation of storage agnosticism, interpreter/database round trips and transactional sync ordering. This bounded prototype replaces full CLI/deployment integration as the next backend task; those product surfaces remain off this increment's doing-list. It shares Quarry, rules, maintains and partition logic, but uses an experimental Postgres store. Native SQL exists only as an explicitly separate comparison workload, not an application escape hatch. SQLite remains the production and local-device store. See [the experiment](./experiments/postgres/README.md).

- **2026-09-15 — the server half of LLP 3000.009 moves onto the doing-list** (author: "begin implementation using astra xhigh to code"; LLP 3000.009 §10.2, increments A1–A3). Unblocks the online-only brief's invitation, webhook and scheduled journeys without a second application server: work records whose authority is bound at admission, ids bound to their operation and arguments, declared action results, a generated handler context with a durable record per external step, declared routes and webhooks, scheduled actions under service seats, and `serve` supervising the runtime. Taken off in the same change: "A jobs framework" narrows — actions, effects and scheduled actions get per-operation retries, deadlines and cancellation; priorities, worker pools and dead letters stay out — and the actions card's "supervise the worker as an ordinary Node process" leaves. Stands: no JavaScript in the data plane, no identity provider in the engine, and no `ctx.sql`, projections or SQL reads in this increment.

- **2026-09-15 — the Postgres access boundary moves onto the doing-list** (LLP 3000.009 §10.2, increment C1). Unblocks measuring Postgres behind a batched access boundary instead of row-at-a-time calls: transaction-scoped prefetch, a pipelined commit tail, one portable index-key bound checked on both stores, and the corpus, conformance and device suites on both stores. Taken off in the same change: the evaluation prototype's single-adapter framing, in which the only next step was an unbatched store. Stands: Postgres is not a hosted or deployable target; its refusals of assets, jobs, actions and schedules, and `serve`, `deploy` and backup on Postgres, wait for increment C3.

- **2026-09-13 — word search, effects, service seats and presence move onto the doing-list** (author: "add all the things we need for Clique and Interview"; the full-brief Clique record's remaining capability lines and Interview's adapter responsibilities). What it unblocks: Clique M5's three search scopes online and offline on one index, M6's push seam without connection leases hand-built in tables, M8's digest emitted rather than logged into a table, Interview's phone sign-in and model workers as a service that mints sessions instead of proxying every request, and the ranked feed of LLP 3000.004 on the `feeds` card. Taken off in the same change: "an embedded JS engine for effects, until measured" (the engine never calls out; a worker does), "word search" from the package's exclusions. Stands: no JavaScript in the data plane, no relevance ranking, no identity provider in the engine.
- **2026-09-12 — expiry, assets and jobs move onto the doing-list** (author: "complete snapback4 to the point where the entire clique benchmark can be run"). What it unblocks: the struck half of the Clique brief (M4 media, M5 view-once and expiry with no client open, M8's scheduled digest). Taken off in the same change: the "one photo path" and "cron syntax" exclusions above narrow to transcoding and to cron beyond `every N [at HH:MM]`; the "time in rules" exclusion stands, answered by `expire at .col` rather than a rule.
- **2026-09-13 — native jobs on (author: "write up an LLP about this and then implement").** Unblocks the scoring half of a feed — floats, vectors, real loops, a typed schema — without putting any of it into Quarry, and gives the model half of LLP 3000.004 a seat inside the bounds: one chunk per commit, the job ceilings, fuel, no I/O. Off the doing-list in the same change: the "external ranker with a service seat" of LLP 3000.004 §6.5 is no longer the only shape for compute over the store; it remains the shape for the network. Ranked and vector search, effects execution and an embedded JS engine stay above.
- **2026-09-13 — `sql`, a fifth named observability verb, moves onto the doing-list** (author ruling on LLP 3000.007 §14.10). Read-only SQL over generated per-table views of the developer's own store, under `dev` or against a `--release` directory; never rule-filtered, never an application surface. What it unblocks: the ground truth two Clique essays said Snapback lacked beside Postgres ("there is no underlying Postgres to query"), and the inspection the 0.2.6 Astra lane did by copying its SQLite file. Taken off in the same change: SQL reads as an application query surface (LLP 3000.007 §9.1) leave the doing-list until the reporting gap is measured after the language relaxations; reporting is Quarry's, inspection is `sql`'s. Stands: no JavaScript in the data plane, one authoring language.

- **2026-09-14 — the simulator witness is published for app builders (LLP 3000.007 §20.10, S30).** Unblocks native offline proof artifacts; replaces the repository-only Metro witness on the doing-list. Real-browser and device witnesses still cover only what the deterministic simulator cannot reach.

- **2026-09-14 — development clock control (S27, calibration F5)** moves onto the doing-list to unblock expiry and scheduled-digest tests without real-minute waits or backdated seeds; waiting on real time leaves the development test path, while production clock controls stay off the doing-list.

- **2026-09-25 — ephemeral tables enact the standing realtime line of LLP 3000.009 §12 (LLP 3000.017).** Unblocks typing and viewing without durable outbox traffic. Nothing leaves the doing-list: this enacts an already admitted scope. Last-seen storage, multi-server presence, a separate presence stream and ephemeral authority over durable invariants remain excluded.

- **2026-09-30 — bounded client subscription inspection (author's PR round-0 task)**
  moves onto the doing-list: query-group queue/in-flight/withdrawal counts and
  bounded latency/cause samples explain mounted screens flashing empty. Taken off
  the doing-list: builders must reconstruct this pressure from ad-hoc console
  instrumentation. General observability beyond the named verbs remains excluded;
  there is no telemetry transport or server admin console (LLP 3000.023 §10).
# Rules

**Snapback 2 optimizes the edit-to-verified loop — the agent's building an app on it, and ours building it.**

Speed is the constraint. Everything below exists to keep the edit-to-verified loop short
and to stop this repository accreting apparatus faster than anyone can remove it. These
rules bind `snapback2/` and the LLPs numbered 1000 and up; Snapback 1 is baselined, not
exempt, and its numbers may only fall.

Each rule is **[check]** (enforced by `caps`) or **[review]** (enforced by a human).

## Budgets — every budget is a trade, not a limit

- **5 blocking checks, 60s total.** A sixth requires deleting one, in the same PR. **[check]**
- **59 documents in the working set** (`llp/current/`). Today's count. A 60th requires
  archiving one — which is just removing the link. Snapback 2 aims to leave this at 15;
  Snapback 1's links are archived at promotion. **[check]**
- **12 documents in the foundation** (`llp/foundation/`). **[check]**
- **This file: 700 words.** If it grows, something becomes a check or stops being a rule. **[check]**
- **1,500 lines per source file, baseline 204.** Generated files exempt — and generated
  files are built, not committed; so are benchmark run records under `benchmarks/runs/`,
  which are evidence, not source. The baseline is Snapback 1's oversize count on the day
  the rule landed; it may not grow, and `snapback2/` contributes zero to it. **[check]**

## Blocking checks

`caps` · `ref-check` · `snapback2/scripts/test.sh` · `snapback2/scripts/measure.mjs --check` · `check-inventories`

## Loop shape

- **Every check reports all failures in one run.** Fail-fast is banned. **[check]**
- **Nothing blocks on anything slower than 60 seconds.** Slow verification runs on a
  schedule, attributed to the breaking commit, and files a bounded set of issues. **[review]**
- **Fix loops get 3 rounds.** Then stop: escalate to a human or descope. **[review]**

## Time budgets

Tracked every run of `snapback2/scripts/measure.mjs` (appends `snapback2/metrics/loop.jsonl`).
A regression is a P0 with a name on it.

| | budget |
|---|---|
| Incremental rebuild after a one-line edit | 15s |
| Test what you just changed | 30s |
| Blocking gate suite | 60s |
| Full build, warm cache | 5 min |
| **`snapback2 check` on a program (the agent's product latency)** | **1s** |
| **`snapback2 doctor` boot-to-ready** | **0.5s** |

## Scope

- **`NOT-DOING.md` is binding.** Moving something onto the doing-list means writing why
  and taking something off. **[review]**
- **Delete; don't deprecate.** No compat shims or migration paths before promotion. **[review]**
- **Nothing is compiled, transpiled, or generated at runtime that could be built ahead of
  time.** **[review]**
- **A spec needs an implementer and a date, or it isn't written.** **[review]**
- **Only implemented specs bind.** A document with no running code behind it is a
  proposal, and a proposal cannot block a PR or justify a check. **[review]**

## Agents

- **Agents remove apparatus freely and add none.** An agent PR that adds a check, script,
  registry, config file, or design doc needs explicit human approval saying so. **[review]**
- **2026-09-06 session exception:** the user delegated the search implementation
  decisions; one `snapback2-core/build.rs` may generate pinned Unicode tables at
  build time, alongside the existing package build. No new gate, registry,
  runtime generator, or Unicode dependency is authorized by this exception. **[review]**
- **Snapback 2 LLPs are numbered 1000 and up**; `llp-create` must be told. **[review]**
- **Snapback 4 LLPs are numbered 3000 and up** (`snapback4/`, LLP 3000); it adopts these budgets
  and loop shape as its own and keeps its scope in `snapback4/NOT-DOING.md`. **[review]**

## Shared machines

- Warm, named lanes, one per stream of work. Never clone fresh; reuse the worktree.
- **Never `git stash`.** Use a temporary commit.
- Kill only PIDs you recorded at launch. Never by name or pattern.

1642:- **A store identity change or a sequence regression is terminal** (`E_STORE_REPLACED`, `E_STORE_RESTORE`) for every entry point: reads, writes, actions, uploads, assets, refresh. `createApp` reopens after a replacement. Pending writes are durably retired, and a retired or displaced id is never admitted again.
1644:- **`createApp` session custody is modelled.** Invariants I1–I13 are checked over 5,000 seeded interleavings on each of the online and local clients. The interleavings mix sign-in, refresh, logout, logout-everywhere, reopen, held responses and injected storage failures. Among the invariants:

    10	
    11	## 1. Commission and constraints
    12	
    13	Wave-15 fix lists 6 and 8 commission production adaptation for custom apps,
    14	offline entry and draft survival. This amends starter §14, client §6.11 and
    15	release-client §3. No storage, sync, session, expiry or authorization protocol
    16	changes. Existing table rules and constraints decide each write. No version
    17	bump or publication belongs to this lane.
    18	
    19	## 2. Readiness and current authority
    20	
    21	useAppReady means the current account's initial opening succeeded; it is not
    22	permission, connectivity or continuing write admission. Once true, it stays
    23	true for that App and signed-in viewer through release/catalog loading and
    24	later admission warnings. Initial admission failure stays false until a later
    25	successful sign-in/opening; a replacement app or account starts a new readiness
    26	lifetime. Signed-out/restoring auth remains false. AppProvider's existing
    27	app/viewer boundary still resets account-owned drafts; no stale identity is
    28	retained to conceal revocation or store replacement.
    29	
    30	The compiled role shell owns drafts and selection above every readiness,
    31	role and release branch. Sticky role is a label only. Protected reads and
    32	submission require a present, complete, fresh, non-revalidating current role
    33	and generated operation availability, as well as initial readiness. Loading,
    34	partial, denied, absent, stale or revalidating role evidence never licenses
    35	protected children or a submission. SDK/server admission still handles races.
    36	A release switch withdraws protected children while preserving the shell and
    37	its inputs. Explicit revocation withdraws authority immediately.
    38	
    39	## 3. Custom production plans
    40	
    41	Preview stays read-only; apply writes only the exact compiled schema shown.
    42	Retain bare identity insertion only when identity is absent. Bare identity
    43	allows passwords and guests; identity guests is guests-only; password removes
    44	guests. A guest session is a session: signup/login can replace it through
    45	app.auth, with the ordinary account boundary; it is not a dev persona.
    46	
    47	Keep existing blocked-roster bootstrap mutations for compatibility. Extend
    48	planning to a table with an enum column named role and principal identity in
    49	a unique key when no mutation/job inserts or upserts that table, even if the
    50	advisory's approximate reachability misses it. Generate one service:ops-guarded
    51	insert operation per declared role, with that role fixed as a literal, rather
    52	than a caller-supplied role argument. Inputs retain all other writable required
    53	columns and explicit principal defaults. Such provisioning is independent of
    54	fixture values; all declared roles appear even when the current fixture uses
    55	only one. Existing writer paths remain application policy; the planner does
    56	not claim their role coverage. Non-roster and inherited-authority roots stay
    57	explicit review messages. Add only the displayed independent service read and
    58	insert branches; never bypass constraints or rewrite an application's screens.
    59	Fresh names avoid collisions; repeated apply is idempotent once writers exist.
    60	The whole plan must compile before any file write and retain full-source
    61	preflight/symlink refusal. A failed proposed policy writes nothing.
    62	
    63	The principal scan excludes every dot-directory, test directories and known
    64	acceptance/fixture/proof/test scripts. Runtime source files under scripts still
    65	get scanned. Findings remain bounded review hints, never a proof of complete
    66	migration. Detect usePersonaApp, persona: and ?as= custom entry sites and point
    67	them at guide production's account/persona recipe; do not rewrite arbitrary UI.
    68	Upgrade names init --adapt --production for a persona-only project.
    69	
    70	## 4. Declaration and recipe corrections
    71	
    72	Post-baseline row and projected fields explain their optionality with
    73	“optional: releases before N lack this column”; N is the existing configured
    74	since_release label, not an inferred server generation. Public argument labels
    75	retain their existing meaning. A literal string arm of a conditional joins as
    76	its singleton enum domain; enum arms retain their union, including nullable
    77	arms. Dynamic text still widens to text; evaluation/laziness and wire values
    78	are unchanged. This is a precision correction, not a new syntax or IR feature.
    79	
    80	Device-less identity createApp calls without sessionStore must produce a type
    81	error naming sessionStore. Device/persona/readOnly forms remain valid. Complete
    82	empty lists already classify as absent; pin that behavior in packed tests.
    83	The client PDF recipe explicitly calls upload with {file:{...}} and executes
    84	against the packed SDK. Other lanes own the literal wire and proof API pages
    85	(KIT4), ordinary starter advice (DAY5), rules recipes (RULES5), and compiler
    86	notes (LANG4).

## Summary

Snapback 4 is one of two parallel candidates for the successor to Snapback 2,
and the sibling of Snapback 3, not its successor. It builds
[LLP 2000.000](./2000.000-keep-the-core-flip-two-substrates.rfc.md) as
written: the Snapback 2 core kept verbatim, two substrates flipped up front —
resources metered at runtime instead of priced statically, and a client that
replicates the viewer's declared partition instead of caching receipted
results — proved first on the hub fixture, with a deterministic simulator as
the primary harness. Snapback 3 ([LLP 2000](./2000-snapback3.rfc.md), led by
Codex / GPT-6 Astra) keeps the same two questions open and discovers their
answers in its first slice. Snapback 2 ([LLP 1000](./1000-snapback2.explainer.md))
continues as the incumbent and the control. All three are judged by one
pre-registered comparison on one clock.

This document records the author's decisions of 2026-09-12 that created the
track, restates the bet on one page so a reader can orient without the brief,
fixes the bakeoff protocol, draws the fence, and plans the first four weeks.
The design argument and its evidence stay in LLP 2000.000. Only running code
will bind (`rules/RULES.md`).

## 1. Decisions (author, 2026-09-12)

On 2026-09-12 Charlie Cheever asked whether Claude Fable 5.1 should lead a
Snapback 4 while Codex / GPT-6 Astra leads Snapback 3 and Snapback 2
continues, "and see which one turns out best." The lead answered yes with
conditions; the author accepted them ("Sounds good") and asked for this
document. The decisions below are therefore the lead's proposal as accepted
by the author. Where the author has not separately ruled, the text says so.

1. **Three tracks, one comparison.** Snapback 2 continues as the incumbent
   and the control. Snapback 3 builds LLP 2000; Snapback 4 builds LLP
   2000.000. This is a bakeoff of *bets* — discover the substrates in the
   slice versus decide them first — not of models. The result will say more
   about the bets than about the leads, and §3 is written so that it can.
2. **The name is Snapback 4.** Siblings, not a sequence: "4" does not mean
   "after 3." A separate number gives the track its own corpus root,
   directory, package identity and data directory with no rename when the
   bakeoff resolves, and files never move in LLP. If Snapback 4 loses, it is
   a number that never shipped. "Snapback 3b" was considered and rejected:
   it reads as a variant of 3, and this is a different bet.
3. **Snapback 4 builds LLP 2000.000 as written.** Its §14 open questions are
   the lead's to decide inside the track, recorded in §7 as they are
   decided. The author rules on the bar, the comparison and promotion (§3).
4. **One owner on the core.** The lead writes the engine, the store and the
   client contract. Codex lanes build the fixture generator, the reference
   applications, the device witnesses and the periphery. Blind reviews from
   two model families are reserved for the two disclosure-sensitive
   mechanisms: partition inversion of read rules (LLP 2000.000 §7.1) and
   the meter's cut behaviour on non-uniform scans (LLP 2000.000 §6). This
   departs from the standing rule that Codex codes and Claude orchestrates,
   deliberately, for the core only.
5. **The clock is four weeks** from the first commit under `snapback4/` to
   the first slice running on the hub fixture. At the clock the author kills
   the track, merges its substrates into a survivor, or continues it. There
   is no third month without a ruling.
6. **Shared and not shared.** Shared with Snapback 3: the LLP corpus, the
   Clique harness and scorers, the fixtures, the briefs, and the simulator
   once either track has one. Not shared: code, except the Snapback 2 pieces
   both are expected to copy (LLP 2000.000 §5.1).
7. **Snapback 2 keeps shipping and freezes new substrate work for the
   duration.** Fix-forward, the loop bar, acquisition and papercuts continue;
   no new certificate axes, client custody mechanisms or ceiling changes.
   This is accepted here as a condition of the bakeoff. Making it binding on
   concurrent Snapback 2 sessions needs one line in `rules/NOT-DOING.md`'s
   Moves ledger, which this document does not write.
8. **Reading load.** This root, updated in place, and one append-only
   journal, `snapback4/JOURNAL.md`. Sub-LLPs only for a decision that must
   outlive the journal. No panels beyond decision 4's two reviews.

## 2. The bet on one page

**Keep verbatim from Snapback 2** (LLP 2000.000 §5.1): the rule language
with its uniformity and hoist analysis, `decided_true` and the
structural-fact refusals; one data language, Quarry, with `--` comments,
`require … else CODE`, `shape`, `merge`, `walk`, `chunk`; the value model
and its conformance corpus; typed read and write states with no silent state
and the refusal envelope `{code, family, site, message, rewrite, guide}`; the
successor classifier (widening additive, checked narrowing, exact projection
ownership); `deliver`'s engine-computed audience, tombstones and

### 6.9 Wave 12 client recipes

Implementer: Codex for Charlie Cheever, SURF, 2026-10-14, target 0.4.29.
Expo exports secureStoreSessionStore(SecureStore), taking the structurally typed
expo-secure-store module. It maps SessionStore get/set/delete to the host's
async methods, propagating every failure. A fixed prefix plus four hexadecimal
digits per UTF-16 code unit is injective even for empty keys, punctuation,
normalization variants and lone surrogates. No sanitizer or truncation may
merge distinct backend or read/full session keys. The app installs SecureStore
and rebuilds; this adapter changes no Swift code, auth custody or native ABI.

The guide names useAuth members from its actual return type; it exposes no
session or bearer. Principal-sharing and trusted enrollment replace persona
names. Production begins with identity plus seat writers for unbootstrappable
root tables before the optional profiles/teams policy. A role query gates the
invitation UI; an absent list may be an authorized empty list, never a role
verdict. Direct enrollment is distinguished from accept/revoke invitations,
whose authorization recipe belongs to RULES2.

Generated query comments teach isReady(read) && read.data and matchRead.
Shared list/write props use PageHook and WriteHook with page.read metadata.
Bare string operations on createClient query/mutate refuse E_OP with the
generated-handle spelling before dispatch. The assets card distinguishes
online upload from device-owned uploadAndPlace custody and waitSeconds: 0.

GET /auth/config includes development true on dev, false on serve. It is
public discovery metadata, not authority: personas and all dev doors retain
their existing server admission checks. SDK auth ownership stays private.
Tests assert both hosts and the actual type/runtime helpers; Swift compiles
on the publication Mac, with no native changes from this workstream.

### 6.10 Wave 13 production transition

The native README, operations reference and both active Expo client templates
use `secureStoreSessionStore(SecureStore)`; historical starter snapshots stay
unchanged because adaptation matches their original bytes. Device-less
identity `AppOptions` requires an explicit SessionStore at compile time. The
type system cannot infer a JavaScript host, so non-browser hosts explicitly
choose `memorySessionStore()` if they need memory-only identity. Persona,
concrete-device and read-only forms retain their existing explicit paths.
Runtime browser admission still verifies journal authority: an arbitrary
structurally compatible store does not grant browser write custody.

`guide production` states that bare identity retains guests and password-only
identity removes them. It includes a transition checklist, combined password
and development-persona entry, roster-gated invitation and shared-principal
controls. `/auth/config.development` controls discovery UI only; server
admission continues to decide authority. `why N_NO_PRODUCTION_WRITER` lists
all diagnostic roots/dependents and proposes seat bootstrap mutations for each
root. A root with a `role` enum gets one mutation per variant. Proposals never
edit files, mint seats or grant roles: authors add the explicit insert-rule
branch and any required probe visibility, check their schema and provision
real account principals. Non-enum role encodings remain application policy.

### 6.11 Wave 13: ready forms and stable account shells (KIT2)

Implementer: KIT2, 2026-10-16; authorized fix lists #10/#11 and #3 readiness.
No authentication, session, disclosure or write-custody protocol changes.
`useAppReady()` under AppProvider waits for this app's initial `ready` to
settle and requires current auth signed-in without an E_ADMISSION-family
refusal. SDK opening failures publish signed-out or an admission refusal;
readiness stays false. Successful later sign-in clears that refusal, so a
transient initial restoration failure never permanently disables the form,
including when the provider mounts after recovery. A new app instance starts
false; a retired promise cannot publish into its replacement. Readiness is not
online status, permission, or an acknowledgement of a later write. Forms/proofs
gate initial writable content on it without interpreting partially opened
persona clients. Surface auth.why while writes cannot be admitted.

AppProvider resets account-owned React state on app identity or viewer changes,
not auth-state text alone. Restoring and signed-out both have null viewer, so
that transition preserves sign-in inputs. Entering or leaving an account still
resets state. A replacement app resets even with the same viewer (including a
backend switch). Starters render editable authentication controls only while
signed-out, restoration placeholders otherwise, and readiness gates writers.

The compiled client card's online role/release form keeps drafts and selection
above loading/capability branches. A sticky last role labels its shell only;
current role evidence and generated availability gate protected child reads and
Save. Denied/absent evidence closes the gate; loading/partial never grants
permission. Resource changes key the shell. Account/backend changes use the
provider boundary. The browser proof exercises withdrawal, generation switch,
explicit revocation, input identity, selections and account/app replacement.

`isPresent(read)` is a pure generic guard for the ready `present` arm and
excludes `data: null`, preserving mixed `Read` unions. Its typed input is a
`Read`, not a nullable read reference; null/undefined references defensively return false. `isReady` still includes
nullable absent/partial. No read classification changes. Missing `denied`
without `otherwise` joins the existing inference diagnostic path and names
`denied: why => …` / `otherwiseAll`, while still refusing the call. All five
handler names remain loading, denied, absent, partial and present. A complete
empty list is absent; otherwiseAll also receives denied. Lists demonstrate
matchReadList explicitly. Since-release optional fields normalize with `??`
at React rendering/Exact property boundaries. A public guest page explicitly
supplies browser session custody and a read-only client; roster authorization
belongs to RULES3, compatibility generation to DAY3.

READY verdicts. The browser proof covers StrictMode, initial failure/rejection
and later sign-in recovery as well as account and release ownership. Prior
